### A discourse on the testing of the stone

<margin>A discourse on the testing of the stone</margin>
Let it also be known that any man who knows how to work with stones of significant weight knows how to obtain a true <term>treasure</term> <note>original: "thesaurum"; often used to describe the successful completion of the Great Work</note> and the making of gold. But if the diminished part is cast out and the perfecting end is missing, then the loss and <unclear>corruption</unclear> of the <term>species</term> <note>the specific nature or identity of the metal</note> appears only in part during the <term>projection</term> <note>original: "projectione"; the final stage where the elixir is cast upon base metal to transform it</note>. (For he desires only the goodness of the work. This other thing is undoubtedly proven.) Then, one must bring forth the work of that same operation which is called "separation" by some. For now, it <unclear>creates</unclear> those rarer things, pure from one another, so that by casting away the base parts, it explains the work within its [proper] limits. And he gave many lines in his section regarding what he proposed about this work, along with others about which Bacus also wrote subtle treatises.

### A discourse on separation

<margin>A discourse on separation</margin>
Know also that the perfection of our work comes not only from the removal of superfluous things, but also from the addition of those things that are lacking, just as the <term>sun</term> <note>original: "sol"; the alchemical symbol for gold and the active, masculine principle</note> does in a certain work of its own which is properly called the "light of lights." In that matter, know the things which we were able to clarify regarding the subtlety of this master. I have said that the perfection of nature is the rejection of the superfluous. Sublimation <note>original: "Subly," an abbreviation for "sublimatio"; the process of heating a substance until it vapors and then solidifies again to purify it</note> removes the superfluous things, because not all things can reach a state of smoothness, nor can all things be impressed to the highest degree.

<summary>This page explains alchemy as a legitimate science that follows natural laws, detailing how base metals like lead and tin relate to noble ones like gold and silver. It emphasizes that "perfection" in the alchemical work is achieved through both the removal of impurities (separation) and the addition of missing qualities, much like the purifying power of the sun.</summary>

### The Song of the Sublime Fountain

There is a **sublime fountain** whose origin lies hidden in the depths;
It flies in the heights, yet is enclosed on all sides by cracks.
From the first supper <note>original: "Ex prima cena". This may refer to the "prima materia" or the initial gathering of substances.</note> it rises through the pleasant dews.
Through the valley <note>original: "Tempe", referring to the Vale of Tempe in Greece, a literary shorthand for a beautiful, secluded valley.</note> and through the feast, the poisons <note>original: "Benena" (venena).</note> are described.
Like the plants of winter, strong and flourishing from the earth,
Upon which stand the pleasing **elephants** <note>In alchemical Bestiaries, the elephant often represents the "Fixed" or heavy, earthy principle that provides a stable base for the volatile spirit.</note>.
If you wish to know that its essence is for me the **water of life** <term>aqua vite</term>,
Let it finally be purged from the dark body <note>original: "Corp mure". Likely referring to the blackness (nigredo) of the raw matter.</note> by the proper rite.
It brings about this through the sweat that is hunted;
It returns the blackened body to a **whitened state** <note>The "Albedo" or whitening stage of the Great Work.</note>.
Let it reject your vow through its whole self,
And it was the drink that drew the whole toward the heavens.
The human through divine things rains down white, then cooked;
Indeed, one warning is a clear keel <note>original: "carina". The author uses nautical imagery, likening the alchemical vessel to a ship.</note>.
I, having been exalted, thus dwell above all things;
Do not seek to read my works unless you have already read [the masters].

---

<margin>+</margin>
This "Good Man" <note>The text refers to Petrus Bonus, a 14th-century alchemist famous for his work 'The Precious New Pearl'.</note> who is called the **Blessed**, calls the **precious animal** a "soul" <gloss>soul</gloss> in his excerpts. Secondly, he says he calls the "animals of the dews" the "glories," which are moved from the first work. The color appears to the eye like **refined gold** <note>original: "obrizum", a term for pure or assayed gold.</note>. Secondly, in the world it is a fluid water; the soul and the spirit <gloss>spirit</gloss> tinge the "sun" <note>In alchemy, the "Sun" (Sol) is the symbol for Gold.</note>, and then the spirit tinges the "moon" <note>The "Moon" (Luna) is the symbol for Silver.</note>. 

Likewise, the "musty wine" is the **Dragon** <note>The Dragon often represents the raw, volatile, or acidic material that must be "tamed" or "killed" to be transformed.</note>, and it is called "sublimated" and "tailed." The **Body** is a Dragon because of the spirit which it consumes; conversely, his **Bird** is his spirit. And the soul <gloss>soul</gloss> and love <note>original: "am'". Likely "amor" or "anima".</note> came out of the mud. 

Likewise, the **perpetual water** is fire, and it is called **quicksilver** <term>argenta viva</term> <note>Mercury, the fundamental liquid metal of alchemy.</note>. Likewise, the "perpetual water" which is hidden by the philosophers <gloss>philosophers</gloss> is the **worm** <note>original: "v'm's" (vermis). A "worm" or "serpent" often refers to the secret spark of life or the "seed" of the metal hidden within the dross.</note>, and it is called the "quicksilver of the philosophers." 

Likewise, the souls of the sun are captured; first is the retention and service of men, and a famous cause of death. **Afterward, perform the resurrection.** Now everything that the philosophers wove is fulfilled, although the remains of the work were completed; and that first part is still that which he said he would give.

**The method of whitening the stone and concerning the ancient books mentioned above.**

The whitening of this stone is a separation through <term>sublimation</term> <note>the process where a solid turns into a gas and then back to a solid, used to purify substances</note>, which is its distillation. The <term>Philosopher</term> <note>likely referring to Aristotle, often called "The Philosopher" in medieval texts</note> in his first summary says there is one way that leads to the <term>spirit</term> of the <unclear>sun</unclear> <note>gold</note>, with the elixir from blackness being the <term>tincture</term> into white; from the whiteness, the <term>tincture</term> into red <note>this describes the traditional stages of the Great Work: nigredo (black), albedo (white), and rubedo (red)</note>. From the soft, it puts on [strength], and through this, it softens the hard. Because it is powerful enough to change one nature into another nature through the nature of the metal: from color to color, from scent to scent, from taste to taste, from weight to weight.

It is, therefore, the <term>philosopher’s</term> stone. And otherwise, the oil that is in it distills gently until the <term>spirit</term> is made and the fire is increased; its <term>spirit</term> is raised and [it takes] the <term>soul</term> <note>the fixed, essential life-force of the substance</note> with an <unclear>open</unclear> color. Receive that <term>spirit</term> and soak it with the water of <term>life</term>; and having distilled it <unclear>again</unclear>, you should soak it and let it labor for ten days [at the] <unclear>weight</unclear> [of] <unclear>boys</unclear> <note>this may refer to "ludus puerorum" or "children's play," an alchemical term for a stage of the work that is easy or proceeds naturally</note> in the stone, like others [do with] the <term>spirit</term> of the stone; and soak it with the water of <term>life</term> with strength upon a marble stone. And afterward, this operation [is conducted] under the first hot sign <note>likely a reference to the astrological sign of Aries or the first degree of furnace heat</note> for ten days so that the <term>spirit</term> may be dissolved; afterward, distill through an alembic <note>a pear-shaped vessel used in distillation</note> and you will have a sharp water. And if it is not enough, then soak with that water seven times. Otherwise, place the <term>spirit</term> of the stone under the first fire, <unclear>continuously</unclear> <unclear>cooked</unclear>, renewing it until it labors or the stone is admitted, and sometimes <unclear>remains</unclear>. Afterward, soak it [with] this water of <term>life</term> in a sublimatory vessel and let it be placed upon the stone and carried; and when it is perfected, then you shall not fear the <unclear>work</unclear> with the <term>fire</term> <note>original: "pyrone", likely derived from the Greek "pyr" meaning fire</note>.

<summary>This page outlines practical alchemical procedures for creating the Philosopher's Stone, emphasizing the use of "aqua vite" (water of life) and quicksilver to transform metals through the stages of blackness, whiteness, and redness. # Here begins the second book of Galen on the elixir of the sun and moon
<note>original: "elixir solis et lune". In alchemy, the Sun represents gold and the Moon represents silver. While attributed here to the ancient physician Galen, this is likely a "pseudo-Galenic" text, as the historical Galen did not write on alchemical transmutation.</note>

The philosophical tree, which is so named,

<margin>
Concerning the little root of alchemy
</margin>

To this place, by this path, it allows with the greatest effort to reveal the hidden and suggestive secrets to their "sons" <note>original: "filijs". Alchemical texts are traditionally addressed to the "Sons of Wisdom" or "Sons of Art," meaning the initiated students.</note> when they wished. Even the most skilled lovers [of wisdom], these supreme doctors, have reported the secrets in my presence by joining them together, faithfully serving their posterity, so that they themselves might worthily work with the mysteries for which they bore a gift from all sacred things; I shall follow these [secrets] purely and truthfully, [avoiding] <unclear>error</unclear>.

### Concerning the little root of alchemy
<note>original: "radiola alchimie". While 'radiola' literally means a 'little ray' or a specific herb (Rhodiola), in this context it refers to the 'little root' or fundamental principle of the alchemical work.</note>

Let us say, then, in the name of God, that the "little root" of this sacred science is the water [extracted] from metals and stones, from calces <term>calcibus</term> <note>Calx (plural calces): The crumbly powder or oxide left after a metal or mineral has been thoroughly heated or "calcined."</note>, and from all the planets <note>In alchemy, "planets" refers to the seven metals: Gold (Sun), Silver (Moon), Mercury (Mercury), Copper (Venus), Iron (Mars), Tin (Jupiter), and Lead (Saturn).</note>, because such things purify, penetrate, and dissolve. Others indeed produce solidification and coloring. I shall first explain the calces, the figures, the terms, and the names, lest the explanation seem <unclear>incomplete</unclear>.

<margin>
Concerning also the egg of the elixir
</margin>

To capture the "eggs," that is the sediment <note>original: "amurca", referring to the dregs or lees of a liquid, such as oil or wine.</note>, which is sufficient; the **Body** of the **elixir** becomes a tincture. Take, therefore, one part of sal ammoniac <term>sale armoniaco</term> <note>Sal ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a pungent salt used as a powerful reagent to clean or dissolve metals.</note>, and <unclear>thimar</unclear>, and alkali salt <term>sale alkali</term> <note>Sal alkali: Potash or soda, used as a flux in melting and for its caustic properties.</note>; from the other parts, let rock salt <term>sale gemme</term> <note>Sal gemmae: Literally "gem salt," referring to pure, transparent rock salt.</note> be extracted, which is very pure and refined, along with already purified alum and the "flower" extracted from those same waters, and burnt and whitened tartar <term>tartarum combustum</term> <note>Tartar: Potassium bitartrate, often collected from wine barrels; once "burnt" or calcined, it was used as a purifying agent.</note>, and the lime [calx] of eggs dissolved with filings. From all these, as best as you are able, let us dissolve each one in equal portions; although you may have amalgamated it twice, as it is. Afterwards, double the eight parts of all. You see clearly the <unclear>hyrosinitas</unclear> <note>Possible corruption of "hydrosynites," a chemical term for a water-based compound.</note>, the celestial body [made] from all these doubled things; and let us reduce everything into a coagulum <term>coagulum</term> <note>Coagulum: A thickened or solidified mass, the result of a chemical "fixing" of a liquid.</note> that is fitting and clean. Finally, one takes that dissolved substance as medicine. By its own half and another, and with the water from the coagulum, let it afterwards be dissolved again so that it dissolves quickly. Through the solution, the coagulum and afterwards with that water by itself, and its own [parts] each dissolved, and afterwards two parts of the coagulum and even a fourth of the water poured back, and with the water by itself and other things—the clouds [vapors] of this work are most uncertain by themselves—one proper water in everything with the **Body**.

### Concerning the Elixir and its Sublimation

One should take "animated" <term>animatum</term> <note>original: "mercurium animatum." Afterward, take equal amounts of these two and grind them with an <unclear>equal</unclear> amount of **silver calx** <term>calce argenti</term> <note>Silver reduced to a fine powder through heating or chemical action.</note> in "water of tartar" <note>A solution made from the crusty deposits found in wine barrels.</note> and salt water. Let them be <unclear>joined</unclear> <unclear>three</unclear> times in sublimation by your <unclear>gentle fire</unclear> for a <unclear>day and a night</unclear>.

And so, with a <unclear>gentle fire</unclear>, lest the <unclear>spirits</unclear> <note>The volatile vapors or gases released during heating.</note> be lost, the substance will be <unclear>separated</unclear> and will <unclear>distill</unclear> through the **alembic** <term>alembicum</term> <note>The lid or "head" of a still, used to catch and condense vapors.</note> which is in the **aludel** <note>original: "alutel," a series of pear-shaped earthenware pots used for the sublimation of solids.</note>, and it must be <unclear>collected</unclear> and <unclear>saved</unclear>. Afterward, that which <unclear>ascended</unclear> into the tube of the **aludel** during your sublimation should be combined with its **dregs** <note>original: "fece," the solid residue or "lees" left at the bottom of the vessel after heating.</note> which remained in the bottom of the <unclear>vessel</unclear>. [Add] the same <unclear>weight</unclear> of its own <unclear>water</unclear>... and it is done. You shall bind it with <unclear>mixed strength</unclear> <unclear>so gently</unclear>. It must be **macerated** with the <unclear>same</unclear> <unclear>water</unclear>, and that which <unclear>ascended</unclear> in the <unclear>sublimation</unclear> together with its <unclear>dregs</unclear> must be **macerated** <unclear>again</unclear>. 

And again, for the <unclear>second</unclear> and <unclear>third</unclear> time, use the fourth part of the **powder of tartar**. Finally, it should be <unclear>placed</unclear> in the <unclear>bottom</unclear> of the **aludel**, and above it, <unclear>everything</unclear> that was <unclear>sublimated</unclear> should be <unclear>placed</unclear> with its <unclear>dregs</unclear> into the **aludel** and <unclear>sublimated</unclear> <unclear>again</unclear>. placed at the bottom. And the **medicine** <term>medicina</term> <note>original: "medicina." In alchemy, this term is synonymous with the Elixir or the Philosopher's Stone in its curative or transformative state.</note> is prepared as you have seen mentioned <gloss>and thus</gloss> with its own water—the water which evaporates by a gentle fire before the sublimations—by macerating the sublimated substance with the dregs <note>original: "fecibus," the solid remains or "dead head" left after distillation or sublimation.</note> and the compound thereafter according to the others. Then the said medicine is "cerated" <term>cerata</term> <note>original: "ceratio," the process of softening a hard substance by adding liquid until it has the consistency of wax (cera).</note> when it is held for dissolving. A good ceration should take the place of the "gift" <note>original: "dotois," possibly a scribal error for "dosis" (dose) or "dotis" (endowment/quality).</note>. Afterward, let the cerated medicine be placed in a **glass vessel** <note>original: "canora," likely referring to a specific shape of glass phial.</note>. Take care that the fire is not with a flame, but should be like **vapor and smoke**. And by the gift of the Lord, every Master or <unclear>student</unclear> in such an art will find that it dissolves sweetly through the solution; let it be left for a time. By changing it from vessel to vessel, it is greatly clarified. After this, let it be well-placed in the known vessel of **coagulation** <term>coagulatio</term>, that is, a **cucurbit** <term>cucurbita</term> <note>The lower, gourd-shaped part of a still.</note> with a "blind" **alembic** <term>alembicum</term> <note>The cap or head of the still; a "blind" alembic has no spout, forcing vapors to condense and fall back down.</note>. And let it be well **luted** <term>lutata</term> <note>Sealed with "lute," a paste made of clay, dung, or lime to make the vessel airtight.</note> at the joint so that it does not "breathe" [leak]. This is so that the evaporation may only descend from above. And through the same vessel, let the medicine be boiled <term>elixatur</term> <note>original: "elixat," a process of long-term heating or decocting.</note> in an earthen vessel which is said to be nearly full, but not entirely. And so it is divided by the artifice which you wish, for otherwise you cannot have the gain. And thus, with the vessel placed over a gentle fire, using a slow heat for as long a time as necessary until the evaporation ceases and the boiled substance is thus held at the bottom of the vessel, **coagulated** in the manner of **alum** <note>original: "lūme," likely *alumine*. Alum was a familiar crystalline substance used as a visual benchmark for successful coagulation.</note>. At the start, the whole thing should have the vessel for 12 hours. Afterward, for another 12 or 15 hours, it should be kept over the heat to thicken.

&nbsp; ...likewise let it be done with the lime of tin <note>calcined or powdered tin oxide</note> which should be macerated with water of sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note> and other alums <note>original: jamenis; likely Yemen alum</note> and alkali, and dissolved, coagulated, and purified three times with lead and calamine <note>a zinc ore</note>; thus macerated with water of sal ammoniac, and thus coagulated. Let the sublimations be made if it is mixed above with the simple lime of silver and pitch to an oil and certified if it proceeds in the true way. Let the stone be made thus from the lime of pure lead purified three times by the nature of calamine. <margin>On the elixir and the red medicine</margin>
<margin>Elixir for gold <note>original: solem</note> and the red [elixir] which tints gold and silver <note>original: lunam</note> into red</margin>
**P**raise be to God who is the origin of all good things from whom all good things proceed. Let us speak, therefore, of the red elixir which is sublimated from red things and red-making things, such as reddened arsenic <note>original: armenicum</note>, saffron of iron <note>iron oxide or rust</note>, and Magnesia <note>an alchemical term for various mineral substances, often manganese</note> which we shall speak of later. This is so that the tincture <note>the coloring property</note> may hold with the red water of iron and with the water of reddened alum by which redness and tinting are mixed into it. Let it receive fixed things <note>substances that resist the fire</note> and remain in itself so that it can stand in the coagulation of fire.

<margin>On the red work</margin>
<margin>On the red work for red [tincture]</margin>
**L**ikewise, according to a certain person, a red elixir is made thus: Take 3 ounces of incombustible sulfur, 1 pound of sublimated mercury, 3 ounces of flower of copper <note>verdigris or copper oxide</note>, 3 ounces of sal ammoniac, 2 pounds of Yemen alum, 3 ounces [more] of sal ammoniac, and 3 ounces of saffron of iron. Let these be ground together and mixed with water of iron and placed in a sealed glass vessel in the furnace steadily through the night; and again soak <note>original: imbibe</note> with resin <note>original: rassa</note> as before and sublimate, and do this until it is sublimated.

<margin>On the water of iron which becomes red</margin>
<margin>On the water of iron which becomes red</margin>
**T**he aforementioned water of iron is made thus: Take 1 pound of the strongest vinegar, or rather 1 pound and 2 ounces more of saffron of iron, 3 ounces of flower of copper, 3 ounces of Roman vitriol <note>iron or copper sulfate</note>, 3 ounces of Yemen alum, 3 ounces of sal ammoniac, 3 ounces of alkali salt, and let them macerate in the sun for 4 days as in the following artifice, and let it be distilled through a filter until it is clear, and soak the aforementioned medicine [with it].

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the preparation of the Red Elixir, specifically focusing on the creation of the "Red Stone" and the "Stone of Gold," which were believed to possess transmutative properties. It follows the previous page's discussion on metallic limes and the initial stages of the red medicine.</meta>

...from which afterwards a red stone is made by coagulation. It shall be prepared so that it is first smeared with double its weight of vinegar, in which vinegar there is dissolved as much as possible of sal ammoniac <note>original: sale ar.; ammonium chloride, used as a flux and cleaning agent</note> and also of alkali salt. And another portion of Yemen alum <note>original: Jamen; a high-quality alum from Yemen used in dyeing and alchemy</note> as much as the alkali, all by stirring and imbibing <note>the process of a solid absorbing a liquid</note> until it drinks up the red [substance], which is verdigris <note>original: erugo; usually copper acetate, but here specifically a reddened variety</note>; then let it be ground with an equal weight of sulfur and placed in a sealed crucible <note>original: catillo</note> <unclear>sealed</unclear> so that it cannot "breathe" <note>hermetically sealed to prevent vapors escaping</note>. And let it be placed in a hot furnace, and now let the pans be well buried, and let it stay there overnight, buried in the hot ash or among the coals, as in a bath or a furnace. And after it has remained there overnight, let it be taken out, dissolved, and ground with double its weight of urine distilled through a filter so that it is quite clear and clean. And then let it be ground for a long time until it is dissolved. Then let reddened borax <note>original: tuncar; also known as tincar, a flux used in soldering and alchemy</note> be added. And with the dissolved reddened borax, let it be well macerated and smeared as prepared before; and after being dissolved, let it be coagulated in all heat until perhaps it becomes a red and clear stone.

### On the stone of gold
<margin>on the stone of gold</margin>
Likewise, let gold be calcined <note>heated until it turns to a powder</note> and mixed with sal ammoniac and the aforementioned borax until it dissolves, and afterwards let it be coagulated until it becomes a stone. Afterwards, let it be dissolved with the reddened substance and sal ammoniac in equal parts in the sharpest vinegar. And let plates of metal <note>original: lamie; thin sheets or foils</note> be heated in the fire and quenched in the aforementioned vinegar, repeatedly placing them inside and quenching them until the plates, by failing <note>breaking down</note>, are consumed within the vinegar. And let it remain in the bottom of the vessel until the vinegar settles; this will be the calcination and pulverization. Take that gold which was dried in the preparation and let it be ground in a vessel or with the aforementioned dissolved borax for ten days, moistening it daily until it drinks up the portion of borax and until it is dissolved. Afterwards, let it be imbibed with the dissolved borax in a quantity equal to the substance itself; and this is done so that the substance recovers its tincture <note>original: tinctur̃; the color-giving property or alchemical essence</note>, and it shall be processed for dissolving, and afterwards let it be coagulated until it becomes a stone.

...two types of salts and alum. And in this water, cook the soft sulfur over a slow fire until the combustible moisture <note>original: humiditas adustiva; the "oily" part of sulfur believed to cause it to burn away too quickly</note> of the sulfur is first driven out, such that if some of that sulfur is placed in the fire, it does not burn. Afterward, take a pound of common salt and dissolve it in two pounds of vinegar; cook the whole mixture for the sulfur to drink up while being ground <note>original: molares; to grind or mill together</note>, and dry those two: the sulfur and the lime <note>original: cala; likely a shortened form of calx, meaning a roasted metallic powder</note>. Once they are well-prepared and dried, they can be placed in a sublimation vessel <note>original: alutel; a pear-shaped pot used in the sublimation process</note> and sublimated <note>heated until the substance turns to vapor and reforms as a solid at the top of the vessel</note>.

Take the sublimated sulfur and also another portion of Yemen alum <note>original: Jamei; high-quality alum from Yemen</note> equal to the pound of sublimated sulfur. Add one dram of sal ammoniac <note>original: sal ar.; ammonium chloride</note> and let it be ground with vinegar in which common salt has been dissolved in the same quantity as before, until it receives its weight of salt and dregs; and afterward, let it be sublimated. 

Afterward, let it be tested by placing it in the fire upon nut oil, and if it does not burn, the oiliness is gone. If it does not lose its oiliness, let it be sublimated again in the same manner until it does lose it. Afterward, let it drink <note>original: bibat; the process of "imbibition" where a dry powder absorbs a liquid</note> with reddened borax <note>original: atincar Rubificato; "tincar" or "atincar" refers to borax used as a flux</note> that has been dissolved, and with dissolved Yemen alum. And let it be ground with all of this until it receives its weight of borax; and let it be made from the prepared substance with reddened birculum <note>possibly a variant of vitriol or a specific mineral preparation</note>, Yemen borax, and sal ammoniac, and in this way let it be sublimated.

### Of the Red Stone

<margin>Of the red stone</margin>

Afterward, let the reddened birculum and sal ammoniac be prepared in double the amount of distilled brine <note>original: brina distillata; likely distilled urine, frequently used as a solvent in alchemy</note>; in this brine, let common salt be dissolved, well-spread, in the same quantity as the birculum. Take also one pound of sal ammoniac and let the whole preparation drink of the aforementioned water until it has drunk it all; finally, let it be ground as many times as needed until dried. 

Afterward, let it be put in a vessel well-prepared with lute <note>original: luto; alchemical clay or sealant used to make vessels airtight</note> and bury it in the coals of a bath or a furnace overnight. Then let it be ground and sublimated. And let it be prepared, ground, and sublimated twice in this way with a fifth part of its weight of the aforementioned dissolved reddened borax; and so let it be sublimated until it becomes red in its sublimation.

### Body of the Elixir of Gold
<note>original: Corpus elixir mi de sole; "Sol" (the Sun) is the standard alchemical symbol for gold.</note>

<margin>Body of the Elixir over gold</margin>

Let it be done, therefore, in the name of the Lord, for the present sublimation. Take one ounce <note>original: $\xi$; a common symbol for an uncia, or ounce</note> of the Red Stone of which we spoke above, one ounce of pulverized salt-stone, one ounce of iron-stone, one ounce of copper-stone <note>original: lapide eris; aeris refers to copper or bronze</note>, one ounce of sulfur-stone, and one ounce of vitriol-stone. Add to this eight ounces of sublimated mercury and eight ounces of the aforementioned sublimated common salt. Let these be ground and combined <note>original: incorporentur; literally "incorporated," meaning to blend into a single body</note> with the "Reddened Water of All"—that is, the water used for reaching perfection. Let there be as much water as the total mass of the materials. 

Grind it again upon a porphyry stone until it is dried and pulverized. Once pulverized, let it be sublimated, starting with a gentle fire until all moisture has evaporated. Let it be distilled through a still <note>original: distillatorem</note> into a glass flask <note>original: ampulla</note>, where it is to be collected; keep it carefully until the night hour. 

And know that if the matter is left to itself, you should take all that remains in the bottom of the vessel. Place the portion that is most intensely red and sublimated by itself, along with reddened earth that has been cleansed in water as previously described. In a similar sublimation, let a layer be made of one part [of the matter] ground with one part [of the earth], and let it be sublimated in this way once, always adding one part of the aforementioned mixed "alman" <note>likely a contraction of "alumen" (alum) or a reference to "sal almanicum" (sal ammoniac)</note>. 

Collect the water that evaporates during each sublimation in a very clean vessel, repeating this ten times. From then on, use the water that distilled from the "dead" <note>original: mortua; materials that have lost their "spirit" or volatile components</note> and sublimated materials. You shall sublimate further with "water of glass" <note>likely a solution of silicates or a specific mineral acid</note> and with "water of copper." Finally, let it be macerated on the porphyry stone. Place it in a dissolution vessel within a water bath <note>original: balneo</note> so that it may be skillfully dissolved. Afterward, it is to be coagulated in a vessel of conjunction. Perform this in a cucurbit <note>a gourd-shaped chemical vessel</note> and a "blind" alembic <note>original: alembico ceco; an alembic without a spout, used for refluxing or "circulating" vapors</note> over a very slow fire, so that the spirit may receive the evaporating moisture with such sweetness that it does not...

<margin>concerning how living water is made</margin>
### Concerning how living water is made
**H**aving set aside [the previous steps], let us place rectified oil. **What was first** [done] in this manner: Let us take 2 ounces <note>The OCR uses "3." or "2.", which likely represents the symbol for an uncia (ounce) or a specific part</note> of sublimated and reddened sal ammoniac <note>original: sale armoniaco; ammonium chloride</note>; let it dissolve and macerate with an equal amount of alkali salt, likewise whitened. And let the whole be dissolved with this water; let it macerate as much as possible, as 3 parts of it are better for the finest gold calcined by itself. Of this water, take 3 parts with 2 parts of [another] water, as 2 parts are better; and let it dissolve and coagulate.

### Concerning rectifying oil
<margin>concerning rectifying oil</margin>
**T**hen proceed by way of a pound of very well-washed hair <note>Human or animal hair was frequently used in early chemistry as a source of nitrogen and sulfur</note>, and 2 ounces of sublimated and reddened sulfur, and 3 ounces of vitriol <note>iron or copper sulfate</note>—all dried—and 3 ounces of dried and pulverized human blood <note>Blood was considered a potent organic "spirit" used to help the minerals "bind" or "vivify"</note>. Let a paste be made from the sulfur and vitriol with the hair and blood, and let them be applied in the manner of a cement. Afterward, let it be distilled with the gentlest fire. And from that oil, let its own weight be added over the [sal] ammoniac which has been made into an alkali sal ammoniac. Afterward, let it dissolve in the sun, in the "earth of the elixir" <note>the solid residue or base of the alchemical work</note> together, and let it macerate in that oil, and let it be dissolved and coagulated. And thus let praise be to God, who is without end and without beginning.

->The book ends.<-

### Here begins the book of the secrets of alchemy
<margin>Here begins the first book of alchemy concerning the Red Work, which must be shown to none except friends</margin>
**Concerning the Red Work, which must be shown to none except friends**
**T**his King—the friend, namely, of the philosophers—is poor, arriving from red mines where he takes his primary origin. For there [one finds] gold, quicksilver <note>original: argenti uiuum; mercury</note>, orichalcum <note>a golden-colored alloy, usually brass</note>, tin, lead, and other species of metals; and also pyrites, azure <note>likely azurite or lapis lazuli</note>, quicksilver, and verdigris <note>original: uiride eris; literally "green of copper"</note>. Saltpeter <note>original: Sal nitrum</note>, sal ammoniac, sulfur salt, green [vitriol?], and salmiac <note>original: almisadir; an Arabic-derived term for sal ammoniac</note>. There are twelve stones, such as magnesia, hematite, coral, crystal, and others of this kind. From these, again, as from a fountain, proceeds the multiplication of the species for dyeing <note>original: tingendi; in alchemy, this refers to the transformation or "coloring" of a base metal into gold</note> into one. Truly, the calcination of the flesh is this, and what follows has been given to this work.

Therefore, let pure gold <note>original: *aurum mundum*</note> be taken and placed in a glass vessel, and let it be dissolved with quicksilver <note>original: *vivo argento*; mercury</note> placed over it and left for 10 days. Afterward, you shall indeed distill a white water; then a saffron-colored water; then a red water. Then you shall distill the white water once again. Lastly, take the remaining dregs <note>original: *feces*; the solid residue left after distillation</note> and, having mixed them with a tenth part of the white water, pour the mass into an earthenware vessel sealed with clay. Let it be placed in a potter’s kiln <note>original: *clibano figuli*; a furnace capable of reaching high, sustained temperatures</note> until, after a long time, it is broken down so that it attains the highest whiteness.

Infuse this mass with the white water and sublimated quicksilver, and roast it over a slow fire in a flask sealed with clay. This same process must be repeated, adding water as much as it can sustain <unclear>and</unclear> [measure]; for in this way, it will congeal into a most excellent powder. One part of this powder fixes <note>original: *firmat*; in alchemy, to "fix" a substance means to make a volatile spirit stable and resistant to fire</note> 60 parts of any "body" <note>original: *corporis*; refers to a base metal like lead or copper</note>.

For it will take on a crystalline clarity. Congeal it, therefore, and it will congeal into a crystalline plate. One part of this plate fixes three parts of any metal body. And if you use this plate with "water of hair" <note>referring to the nitrogen-rich distilled liquid from the hair mentioned on the previous page</note> at its own weight and at <margin>3rd chapter</margin> the third "head" <note>likely a reference to the third stage of distillation</note>, one part fixes seven. And if you dissolve more than you have watered—which will happen in 60 days—it must be congealed, for one part fixes ten parts of any metal body.

### Third Chapter

Chapter Three concerning the same. By adding just as much red water, it will soon dissolve and congeal, and it will take on the color of alabaster. One part will change ten parts of any body into gold.

# Another preparation of the same
You shall place broken borax <note>original: *boraxum*; used as a flux to help metals melt</note> to the height of one palm <note>a measurement of roughly 3-4 inches</note> under an alembic <note>a distillation apparatus</note>. You shall distill it, reserving the water and the oil separately. Afterward, you shall calcine <note>heat to a high temperature to reduce to powder</note> the dregs with salt water in a furnace until it becomes white. Then you shall moisten these dregs with its aforementioned oil, grinding it thoroughly <note>original: *pisando contra drictum*; likely meaning until a smooth or "right" consistency is achieved</note>. Afterward, you shall roast it as if in a flask with the fire of the furnace. For it will congeal into a Red plate, one part of which will transmute ten parts <note>of base metal into gold</note>.

# Another said preparation of the same
In this place, [take] this lime with the aforementioned white substance until ground to dryness. Prepare it for dissolving. Furthermore, you shall add its oil to the water and dissolve it again. It will be dissolved as a Red substance. Afterward, it will congeal into a Jacinth <note>original: *Jacinctum*; a reddish-orange gemstone, likely zircon or a similar-looking alchemical glass</note>, its color being changed. And finally, they suffocate <note>original: *suffocant*; to seal off from air or quench</note> it with its water, and you shall moisten it little by little. And you shall congeal it again, and likewise with the white substance until the whole is moistened and you dissolve it. It will be dissolved into a Red substance. And whatever water was added to it, at last you allow it [to absorb] its heat, dissolving it for forty days; afterward, you shall congeal it with the fire of the furnace. It will congeal into a Red substance, one [part] of which transforms a pound of "bodies" <note>base metals</note> into the sun <note>original: *solem*; gold</note>.

# For the juice of pearls
[Take] one hundred mechal <term>mechal: also known as a miskal or mitqal, an Arabic unit of weight approximately equal to 4.25 grams</term> of Greek [stone?], one hundred of white shells, and 100 of spaltatinde <term>spaltatinde: likely a corrupted form of asphaltum or a specific bituminous mineral</term>, and 100 mechalos of lime made from it, and 20 mechalos of the sea <note>likely sea salt</note>, and one mechal of putrid earth. Afterward, with the aforementioned hail <note>small granules</note> and ivory and masses with horns <note>possibly animal horn or a horn-shaped vessel</note>, you shall grind them until they become smooth. Then you shall place them in a pot in the kiln <note>original: *clibano*</note> and dry them with the sufficiency of the furnace, and it will become a Red fragment. And it is a Red Jacinth. It yields to none in weight and color. Except for the diamond, it is not harmed by weight <note>meaning it is extremely hard and heavy, like a real gemstone</note>.

# Said preparation of the same
Boil fresh borax with a small amount of alum and salt in water. Afterward, you shall calcine it like cuttlefish bones <note>original: *ossa sephias*; often used as a source of pure calcium carbonate in historical chemistry</note>, which you shall prepare with the aforementioned alum. Likewise, the dregs cooked in their own white water with distilled salt water, you shall calcine in the kiln.

Then take another vessel in which there are two <unclear>curie</unclear> and two <unclear>stems</unclear> and <unclear>azarmit</unclear> and <unclear>lebris</unclear>, and leave it in a warm, gentle heat for a <unclear>natural</unclear> day, and it will be hard and <term>malleable</term>.

Take a <term>vial</term> <note>original: *fiala*; a small glass bottle</note> of <unclear>glass</unclear> and put <unclear>honey</unclear> of <unclear>zarbuch</unclear> into it, and put it with the cooked <term>liquid preparation</term> along with a <term>marble stone</term>...

<meta>This page continues the experimental recipes for the transformation of metals, specifically focusing on the "mortification" of mercury and the creation of "fixed" powders using iron (Mars) and tin (Jupiter).</meta>

### 7. The Method of Gold
With **gold** <note>original: *auro*</note> it is made with that which is mortified <note>In alchemy, "mortification" refers to the process of stripping a substance of its outward form, often through corrosion or heating, to prepare it for transformation.</note> by its own nature. Then place it in an <term>aludel</term> <note>original: *aludel*; a series of pear-shaped pots used in sublimation processes</note> and put it in its furnace, and it will become a red powder. Dissolve this according to the "greater lead." Then, using the powder of <term>alutan</term> <note>original: *alūtan*; likely alum-tanned leather or a powder derived from alum-treated materials, used here as a sealant</note>, which is better than all others, seal the aludel. Place it in a hot furnace for at least a day and a night; it will be coagulated and good.

### 99. A Method Better Known Than All Others
Take one pound of the **Stone of the Abyss** <note>original: *lapide abissū*; likely a poetic or specific name for a dark mineral or a type of vitriol</note> and three pounds of <term>alkali salt</term>. Also, take three pounds of the "prior wine" <note>likely a distilled spirit or strong brandy</note> and one pound of strong <term>caprello</term> <note>likely a variant of *capreolo*, possibly referring to a specific salt or a vine-tendril extract</note>. Use two parts of <term>lye</term> in which three ounces of the other is dissolved. From the other part, take one pound of common salt in very sharp vinegar, and half a pound of whitened **tartar**. 

Then place these in a strong, <term>luted</term> <note>original: *lūtata*; sealed with "philosopher's lye" or clay to make it airtight</note> pot and leave it for [number unclear] days under **dung** <note>alchemists used the steady, gentle heat of rotting horse manure to incubate their mixtures</note> with that prepared stone. Then cook it all until a third part is consumed. Let it settle to the bottom and strain everything through a thick cloth. Distill it once through a filter. 

Then combine two parts of this "water" with a third part of the salt of lead until it is totally calcined into **aucharit** <note>an obscure technical term, likely a corruption of an Arabic alchemical term for a specific elixir or "alkahit"</note>. Extract this three times until it is finished. What remains in the mass will have a **fixed, perpetual property**. Use this over fifty parts of prepared **Mars** <note>original: *martis*; the alchemical name for Iron</note>. Then, one part of this "Mars" substance falls upon fifty parts of prepared **Jupiter** <note>original: *iovis*; the alchemical name for Tin</note>.

### 101. The Method of the Two Hundred
Make a sound vessel from the aforesaid materials with its cover, and let it melt... let the iron melt at the top. Then place half a pound of *aucharit* into two-thirds of the "minor stone" or the prepared stone. Close the lid of the aludel and place it in the fire for a day and a night; a good **coagulation** will occur, just as in the method described above. 

**Then:** In the same way, make a small hole in the top part. Then coat the whole phial. Put the "water of the sound vessel" inside, and close the "egg's hole" <note>alchemists often called their oval glass flasks "philosopher's eggs"</note>. Cover the whole egg everywhere with the same paste and place it in the aludel with the coagulated medicines. Heat it for a day and night according to the order described before.

### 129. [Further Method]
The method is as above. It should be cleansed and [unclear] with raw heat; wash it with water and dry it. Afterwards, fix it with oil and...

...with **alkali salt** <note>original: *sale alkali*; an alkaline salt, often potassium carbonate or sodium carbonate derived from plant ashes</note> and pour it from there. And then grind it with water in the stated manner. And place it all in a good **bladder** <note>Animal bladders were commonly used as flexible, waterproof containers for slow heating or digestion in a water bath.</note>. And place that bladder over boiling water for a day until it is dissolved. And then decant that water and **calcine** <note>original: *calcina*; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder</note> it in a crucible. And it will become a great **elixir** <note>original: *elixir*; in this context, a medicinal or transformative powder used to change the properties of metals.</note>.
(Upon 1 part of the prepared substance, add 10 parts of prepared **Venus** <note>original: *venis*; the alchemical name for copper</note>, as Duccai testifies).

Item, the 13th method for itself, from **Avicenna** <note>A Latinized reference to Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian polymath whose works on medicine and chemistry were foundational to European alchemy.</note>. Take the **salt of Jupiter** <note>original: *Sūp. Jovis at sacm̄*; Jupiter is the alchemical symbol for tin; "sacm" likely refers to *saccharum* (sugar/salt) or a sacred preparation.</note> and of the water in which a weight of **mercury** <note>original: *zaybach*; from the Arabic *zaybaq*</note> is placed. Item, the 14th method is thus: Take **arsenic** and [salt] and **alum**. And place them in vinegar in which salt is dissolved. Then act with that vinegar for 3 days with those medicines. And then [squeeze and filter] and place it in the **place of congelation** <note>a cool or specific area of the laboratory where liquid substances are allowed to harden or crystallize</note> as the order proceeded for a natural day, and it will be congealed and white.

Item, the [second] 14th method is thus: Take **mercury** <note>original: *zaybach*</note> in whatever quantity you wish with suitable water for 10 days. And then draw it out and place it in the place of congelation for a natural day, and it will be a good work.

Item, the 15th method is thus, and it is entirely an **experiment of the Indians**. Wash the **mercury** <note>original: *auchariū*; a variant term for quicksilver</note> with salt and vinegar and mustard. Afterward mortify it with salt and alum and vinegar by drying it three times, and place it in the place of congelation as the order proceeded with the medicines that congeal it into white and into a shining work.

Item, the 16th method, always according to **Architlaus**: mortify the **mercury** <note>original: *ducharū*; another variation of the name for mercury</note> again with salt water in which alum has been dissolved, and likewise dissolved by soaking and drying seven times in only one day; place it in the place of congelation for one day in the **furnace of Venus** <note>the copper-smelting furnace, or a furnace kept at a specific heat associated with copper</note>, where, according to others, things are congealed. And it will be a hard white mass of value. Then return it into a substance with the medicines that reduce value into substance.

Item, the 17th method is thus: Then, with water, grind **marcasite** <note>original: *mar dhasitū*; iron pyrites or other metallic sulfides</note> and grind **roasted salt** as much as there is in total; afterward **sublime** it, and grind the sublimated material with water of **egg whites** which has been extracted.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for alchemical "congealing" or stabilization of substances, following the 13th method of Avicenna mentioned on the previous page. It transitions from a method involving lime and salts to a complex procedure involving "the eagle" (sal ammoniac) and "living sulfur."</meta>

...from the **lime** <note>original: *calce*; a powder produced by heating a substance</note> of shells with **sal ammoniac** <note>original: *sale armoniaco*; ammonium chloride, used to clean metals and in sublimation</note> and **alum** <note>a mineral salt used as a fixative</note>. And thicken in that a very white mass, equal parts of the aforesaid. Let it stand for a day and a night and roast it until it is **congealed** <note>original: *geletur*; turned from a liquid or powder into a solid mass</note>. And it runs over a plate of bronze. One part of this turns 30 parts of mercury into **silver** <note>original: *luna*; literally "the moon," the alchemical name for silver</note>. Here ends the method of the way of strengthening.

First, prepare the **alparida** <note>original: *alparidam*; likely a reference to a mercury or arsenic compound like orpiment</note> as you know. After that, take 8 parts of the **fixed** <note>original: *fixaciū*; a substance made resistant to heat</note> **sublimate** <note>a solid deposit produced by heating a substance into vapor and then cooling it</note> and 3 parts of the **eagle** <note>original: *aquile*; alchemical code for sal ammoniac, so named because it "flies" or sublimes easily</note> and **sublime** <note>heat a substance until it turns to vapor and then becomes solid again</note> it 4 times in a glass ampule. Afterward, grind it on the stone and dissolve it with warm distilled vinegar so that it is well thinned. After this, place it in a certain vessel with vinegar and stir it for a long time with a rod, and let it settle for a long time. 

Then distil it through a filter. And what remained at the bottom of the vessel, dry it again over hot ashes. And to this dried substance, add again its part of **sal ammoniac** <note>original: *amuxatii*; a variant term for sal ammoniac</note> and grind it for a long time on the stone. And again dissolve it as before with warm vinegar. Do this until it is all dissolved in the vinegar. Then **coagulate** <note>thicken into a solid</note> it in a glass vessel in the aforementioned ashes.

And what has been settled, sublime in a vial, and then the sal ammoniac will ascend and the fixed substance will remain at the bottom. Then indeed take 1 part and 2, dissolve in vinegar, and mix with the dissolved bronze another part of the sal ammoniac which remained in the vial, grinding it on the stone. 

Afterward, dissolve it in a pot in a **water bath** <note>original: *balneo*; a "bain-marie" or heated water container for gentle, even temperature</note> as it is said (to which dissolved substance add again a part of the **oil of sulfur** <note>original: *olei de alkibrit*; sulfur was often called *alkibrit* in Latin alchemy, derived from Arabic</note> and it is said, thus it is done). Take two measures of the **caustic lye** <note>original: *cupitello* or *capitellum*; a sharp, alkaline water used for dissolving minerals</note> and take 1 pound of **living sulfur** <note>original: *sulphis viui*; native, naturally occurring sulfur that has not been processed by fire</note>. And with one half of this lye, boil the sulfur in a certain pipe that is well-**luted** <note>sealed with airtight clay or wax</note> so that it boils, and boils gradually while watching and feeling it, and continually moving it with a stick so it does not stick to the bottom. 

And when you see that the water is so well reddened and tinted, remove it from the fire and let it settle. Then distil it through wool and save it. Again, prepare another pound of the aforesaid lye which was poured over the sulfur, and boil as before; and when the water is red, distil it, and add it to the other previous red water. Put this red water thus distilled into a glass vessel for congealing and place it in a cauldron with water for congealing as written above.

...and you will know well in all things. Wherefore take 1 or 3 parts of this medicine upon 1 part of **gold** <note>original: *auri*</note> and it makes it like **butter** <note>original: *butirum*; meaning soft and malleable</note>. 

Another [method], more noble. Take the oil which you spoke of above, pour it out and give it <unclear>heat</unclear> to dissolve without earth; afterward, congeal it. Of this congealed [substance], cast 1 part upon 1 part of gold and it makes it soft [to serve] for **mercury** <note>original: *mercurio*</note>. And give it a plentiful fire.

Let it dry for 13 days; afterward, let it drink the water of **sal ammoniac** <term>armoniaci</term> <note>original: *armoniaci*; ammonium chloride, used as a powerful flux and cleansing agent</note> until the stone survives; of this stone, use 1 part for 30, and it produces **refined gold** <note>original: *aurum obrizum*; pure, high-quality gold</note>. **Imbibe** <note>soak a solid in a liquid until absorbed</note> the **sublimated** <note>purified by being turned into vapor and then back to solid</note> substance with the water of **morning dew** <term>lacrimis roris</term> <note>original: *lacrimis roris*; literally 'tears of dew,' considered by alchemists to be a pure, celestial form of water</note>. Soak the pitch alone with it.

<meta>This page continues the technical alchemical instructions from the previous leaf, which discussed the stabilization of precious metals. The focus here shifts to the "rectification" of arsenic and sulfur and the process of whitening (the *albedo* stage of the Great Work). It maintains the use of "The Eagle" as a code for sal ammoniac, consistent with the previous page’s mention of *armoniaci*.</meta>

**Arsenic** <term>arsenicum</term> and **sulfur** <term>sulphur</term>, just as **the water of the eagle** <note>original: *aq quile*; likely a solution of sal ammoniac (ammonium chloride), which alchemists called "the eagle" because of its ability to cause substances to "fly" or sublimate</note> dissolves the **body** <note>the metal being worked upon</note> and will rectify them; however, so that it may have greater efficacy, add **vinegar prepared with spirit** <note>original: *acetum spmo paratum*; likely a concentrated or distilled vinegar</note>. 

Also, I say: take 3 or 4 [parts] of **wine** <unclear>vin</unclear> and dissolve in it 3 parts of **borax** <unclear>B</unclear>, 2 parts of **the eagle** <note>sal ammoniac</note>, and of **white arsenic** <unclear>azermec albo</unclear> 2, 1, or 3 [parts]; let an **alkali** be made. All these can be mixed with vinegar; add **powdered oil** <unclear>oleo pdr</unclear> and place it **under manure** <note>original: *sub fimo*; alchemists used the natural heat of decomposing horse manure as a steady, low-temperature "incubator" for their flasks</note> for ten days. Afterward, take it out and **calcine** <note>heat to a high temperature to reduce to a powder</note> it to a **dry crust** <unclear>scabz</unclear> and give it to the **spirits** <note>volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic</note> to "drink." For this makes them **fixed** <note>original: *fixos*; stable in fire and no longer prone to evaporation</note> and cures them; and if it melts alone by reason of this, [pour] over it some of this oil so that the oil overcomes it, and it will wonderfully rectify the spirit.

### How it is Altered and Whitened

How it may be altered and whitened: Let it be altered through **whitening** <note>the *albedo* stage, symbolizing purification and the transition toward the final goal</note>. First, grind it with **caustic lye** <term>aqua capitello</term> <note>original: *aqua capitello*; a strong alkaline solution used for cleansing</note> and then [expose it] to the **sun** for nine nights, prepared by that means. And add as much as one **vapor** <unclear>vporz</unclear> of **the eagle** <note>sal ammoniac</note> and **sublimate** <note>heat until it turns to vapor and solidifies again at the top of the vessel</note> it; then reduce the sublimated material to a **solid substance** <note>original: *carnem*, literally "flesh"</note> beforehand. And continue sublimating so many times until it rises up **white**. 

Take what has been calcined here through one [step] and [work] with its body through **gold**. Afterward, take **the eagle** <note>sal ammoniac</note> and dissolve it above, and **imbibe** <note>soak a solid in liquid until absorbed</note> **calcined silver** <term>lunam calcinatam</term> <note>original: *lunam*; literally "the Moon," the alchemical name for silver</note>, and dry it until it turns white. 

Afterward, cast the **prepared metal** <unclear>brevem et bellatum</unclear> into the fire and knead it with **oil of egg yolks** <note>original: *oleo de gemis ouoz*; oil extracted from boiled eggs, often used to soften metals</note>; for it softens and makes it **malleable**. 

Also, [take] the water that came out during the **reddening** <note>the *rubedo*, or final stage of the work</note>; dissolve the body in double its weight of **strong vinegar**. Distill it and imbibe with it whatever you wish. Also, dissolve the **eagle** <unclear>agela</unclear> afterward. Also, cast one part upon ten parts and it **tinges** <note>permanently changes the color of the metal</note> it. And if it should be the **lime of gold** <note>gold reduced to a powder by chemical means</note>, it perishes in the night—and by the goodness of God. 

[Blessed] and [broken?], and with **red vitriol water** <unclear>aq zegri rubea</unclear> work with the body as it is completed. <margin>145</margin> Note that this **lime** <note>powder</note> exists by itself, in which the body of **silver** <note>luna</note> is made with moderate combustion; for through **sulfur** it corrupts the body and removes it. 

Then, when the **gold** <note>original: *sol*; literally "the Sun"</note> [releases] its spirit through this, which alone you ought to dissolve... let it become a spirit, and let no trace of the spirit be found in the body afterward. Otherwise, you labor in vain. [This is done] quickly and easily through the mixture of things that cooperate for the **completion** <unclear>plucom</unclear>. 

Note the **differing sayings** <unclear>disimilecs disctoz</unclear> of the wise men regarding the **moistures** <unclear>humes</unclear>... because there is **mercury** <term>argentum vivum</term>, and **permanent water**, and the **dissolved body**, and others, and spirits. And there will come a more [suitable] preparation of the spirits—of them alone—and to imbibe with them the **washed spirits**, so that they may rise and be **sublimated** within the bodies.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the requirements for the "Great Art" (alchemy/natural philosophy). The text focuses on the distinction between the ignorant masses who fail and the "Sons of Philosophy" who succeed through patience and physical labor. The Vatican library stamp mentioned in the metadata partially obscures some word endings, but the context remains clear.</meta>

For many who are ignorant and lack the knowledge of the learned in natural philosophy <term>natural philosophy: the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern chemistry and physics</term> thrust themselves into this Great Art. Finding writings about matters they do not understand, and in which they find no tangible results in their own hands, they become entangled in error and come to despise this science. For such people, it is not only easy but almost certain they will go astray, as the Philosopher <note>original: "philosophus"; usually referring to Aristotle, the ultimate authority for medieval scholars</note> says: for when one proceeds in all things with an equal pace <note>implying a lack of specialized discernment</note>... 

For this work is reserved for those who are worthy of these hidden secrets, of whom there are many <note>The Latin "multi" (many) is unusual here; most alchemical texts claim the worthy are "pauci" (few). This may be an encouraging address to the reader</note>. Therefore, this is not intended as mere dogma <term>dogma: a set of principles laid down by an authority as incontrovertibly true</term>, but so that the Sons of Philosophy <term>Sons of Philosophy: a traditional term for initiates into the secrets of alchemy</term> might be enlightened—not those who are poorly prepared, but those who are ready. 

For one must think more deeply than one speaks. He who follows this path by emotion alone will only lose himself; he must instead be thoroughly hardened within. Let them experience all things gradually, until little by little they are nurtured by the sweetness and the passion for the work. 

For while no one engaged in such pursuits can reach the peak of happiness without effort, the few should be encouraged not to cease their actions. They must persist with the utmost dedication, not only through manual labor but through diligent study, applying themselves even more to balanced and varied methods. 

Thus you may effectively grasp what you seek. Do not be deterred from this task or from the labor of your hands <term>labor of hands: the physical, experimental work in the laboratory, as opposed to pure theory</term> by unexpected difficulties; for at the end of the road, one overcomes through patience and attains that which was desired. From this manual labor, one gains both profit and a nobility of inestimable value. Henceforth, he shall lack for nothing, for he will possess the secret itself.

<summary>This page serves as an exhortation to the aspiring alchemist, warning that the "Great Art" is often despised by the ignorant while encouraging the "Sons of Philosophy" to combine deep study with persistent manual labor and patience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from a discussion on the difficulty of the "Great Art." The author concludes their introductory remarks about their own lack of resources before transitioning into a detailed elemental analysis of metals—specifically lead, tin, and iron. This is a common alchemical framework where metals are viewed as having "complexions" or temperaments, much like human bodies.</meta>

Since on this occasion, being deprived of the labor, means, and funding required, I have stopped; for the future, I shall not consider beginning again, nor arrange to complete what I have started, until the help of grace arrives according to my merits.

### On the complexion of lead
<margin>On the complexion of lead</margin>
Lead, in its surface appearance <term>surface appearance: literally "height" (altitudo), referring to the outer, visible characteristics of the metal</term>, is more silvery and soft, lead-like, melancholic <term>melancholic: in medieval science, the temperament associated with Earth; it is cold and dry</term>, cold and dry, heavy, cold, and black. In its inner depths <term>inner depths: literally "profundity" (profundum), referring to the hidden "soul" or inner essence of the metal</term>, it is red gold, sanguine <term>sanguine: the temperament associated with Air; it is hot and moist</term>, hot and moist, joyful <note>original: "etum"; likely a scribe's shorthand for "laetum" (joyful), a quality of the sanguine humor</note>, sweet-smelling, and masculine. On one side, it is lead and of a watery, leaden nature—cold and moist, white, thickened, relaxed, and feminine. On the other side, it is nearly fiery, choleric <term>choleric: the temperament associated with Fire; it is hot and dry</term>, hot and dry, red, bitter, sharp, and masculine. It is also solidified more easily through digestion <term>digestion: the alchemical process of applying steady heat to "ripen" a substance</term> than [it is converted] into gold.

### On the complexion of tin
<margin>On the complexion of tin</margin>
Tin, in its surface appearance, is more silvery, watery, lead-like, cold and moist, white, thickened, relaxed, and feminine. In its inner depths, it is lead in a fiery, choleric state—hot and dry, red, bitter, sharp, and masculine. On one side, it is lead like silver, and it is melancholic, cold and dry, black, sharp, heavy, and feminine. On the other side, it is red gold, sanguine, hot and moist, joyful, and sweet-smelling, and masculine. And it is more suited for digestion into gold than anything else—except for ices <note>original: "gelis"; likely referring to crystallized salts or "frozen" chemical states</note>—because it is quicker for it to become silvery on the side than in its depth.

### On the complexion of iron
<margin>On the complexion of iron</margin>
Iron, in its surface appearance, is of the complexion <term>complexion: the specific balance of the four qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) within a substance</term> of iron; it is fiery, choleric, hot and dry, red, bitter...

I wish, however, that it not be considered of gold alone, insofar as in their dominion gold alone is the best (just as in every other species of silver <note>here "argentum" likely refers to the "metallic" species in general</note> it is restored by learning, whether it matures or is obscured).

And again, that man is of a base mind who previously opposed this judgment. I wish also that he mentions quicksilver in this account. In the preceding mass <note>original: "menses/messe"; could refer to a "month" of heating or a physical "mass" of material</note>, whether it be graded or not... it is common that then with spirits and now with bodies... the spirits should reasonably flee from that which has a corrupting constriction <note>original: "angustia"</note>, and which leaps into condemnation.

<margin>On the generation of quicksilver</margin>

You should leave the <term>Sun</term> <note>original: "Solem." In alchemical shorthand, the Sun refers to gold or the perfected metallic principle</note> in the first <unclear>month</unclear> in a well-sealed glass vessel, so that it does not breathe, to be soaked for 3 weeks; and let it be changed so that before six weeks it is renewed <unclear>ten</unclear> [times].
When the number is complete, let the <term>Sun</term> be placed <unclear>freely</unclear> from the liquid into another salt, well-arranged with two receivers—one for the other and another for the <unclear>two</unclear>—well-sealed with the **lute of the mastery** <note>original: "luto magisterii," a specialized clay or paste used to hermetically seal glass joints against high heat and pressure</note> for 3 hours over a free fire; afterwards, let the fire grow stronger little by little until all of it is white water. Having distilled [this], the philosophers call this water **virgin's milk** <note>original: "lac virginis," a common term for a white, milky solution, often a precipitate of lead or mercury used as a solvent</note> and the mercury of quicksilver, the **milk of the Lord**, and **water of life** <note>original: "aqua vite," or aqua vitae. While often meaning alcohol, in this context it refers to a "living" solvent capable of dissolving metals</note>, and the water that generates all things present and illuminates the house; and this candle is lit, and all darknesses are put to flight from the house in which it was built, until the end with all its substance—that is, the divine water and the fire in the earth, **calcined** <note>original: "calcinata," the process of reducing a solid to a powder through intense heat</note> for 40 days.
Likewise, let the mercury of the sun be soaked for a week, and you shall distill [it] as before; and do this also after the first soaking, by soaking and distilling. What I have done, let it distill everything in a vessel with ashes <note>A "sand bath" or "ash bath" used to provide even, indirect heat to glass vessels</note> 3 times; afterwards, store it in a glass vessel.

<summary>This page provides instructions for a major alchemical operation involving the "Sun" (gold) and mercury, citing the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus to explain the process of distillation. It details the creation of "virgin's milk," a critical solvent described in mystical terms as a light that dispels darkness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "Great Work" initiated on the previous page. It moves from the philosophical separation of elements to the practical laboratory process of distilling and whitening the substance using spirits of wine and specialized seals.</meta>

For your work well-sealed with wax, and in the water of the <term>water of life</term> <note>original: "aqua vite," referring to highly distilled alcohol or "spirit of wine," used here as a solvent.</note> distilled once; afterward, a small amount of <unclear>tartar</unclear> <note>A salt-like deposit from wine casks, often used in alchemical purification.</note> added to the water before joining that vial. And if the wine whitens [the matter]... and if not, lead it back to the first work until it becomes like <unclear>oil</unclear> in the manner previously stated. 

Let it be made with [preparation] and distillation, and also <unclear>closed</unclear> so that it becomes white; and through testing, let it be known from the matter that it may be whitened as much as possible. Keep for your work the borax of <unclear>tartar</unclear>, and in a vessel well-<term>luted</term> <note>sealed with a specialty alchemical clay to prevent the escape of vapors</note>... and then it is in the water of life because [the remains] from the remainder in the first vessel <unclear>also</unclear> from the mother <note>likely the "matrix" or the source material</note>; which is said to proceed thus in a white <unclear>glass</unclear> vessel prepared with a square of pure stone of sand. It is said [to be placed] in a change so that the vessel is not harmed, but it is said [the vessel should be] well-baked. Distribute that [matter] diligently with a cover, and in that [vessel] in which three holes are prepared in the <term>potter's white kiln</term> <note>original: "albano figuli." The potter's furnace provided the steady, high heat necessary for alchemical calcination.</note>. 

Leave it for [some] days, lest it take up too little heat. Take one part of this water of life and rectify it 6 times, then join it until it fills two parts. 

Place the **Alembic** <note>The upper part of a still, used to capture and condense vapors.</note> above with two receivers, the joints closed with the **master's lute** so that it does not breathe <note>meaning the vessel must be perfectly airtight so no spiritous vapors escape during heating</note>. Place it in a deep vessel full of earth, then let it be placed in a high vessel with water and... for a whole week, or until the water in the distillation is cooled by day and night. Leave it, and casting back into the first vessel, there is a very thin plate.

<summary>This page provides technical instructions for distilling and whitening a substance using "water of life" (distilled alcohol) and tartar. It emphasizes the use of an airtight alembic sealed with "master's lute" and the precise application of heat in a potter's furnace.</summary>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "Great Work." The previous page discussed the whitening of the matter using spirits of wine and a potter's furnace. This page shifts to the preparation of "Physical Water"—a universal solvent—and the purification of sal ammoniac.</meta>

Return to it its own water which was distilled from it until it drinks it all. 
Afterward, save it until you are told otherwise. **On the Physical Water**

<margin>On the Physical Water</margin>
**T**ake <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "salem armoniacum." A volatile salt (ammonium chloride) used by alchemists to "open" or dissolve metals.</note> that has been sublimated by itself, dissolved and distilled through an <term>alembic</term> <note>A classic alchemical still consisting of a gourd-like vessel and a cooling cap with a beak.</note>, then coagulated and once again dissolved and distilled. [Repeat this] as many times as needed until it has been dissolved with <term>water of life</term> <note>original: "aqua vite," or distilled alcohol.</note> that has been distilled seven times, then coagulated, and finally dissolved. It dissolves all <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to powder by heat</note> and burnt bodies, and it dissolves all sublimated and calcined spirits into a single white [mass] in a small vessel over the fire in one hour, with the help of God and a burning spirit. Likewise, my physical water is prepared with God's help and His power. Take 6 ounces of sal ammoniac, dissolved and distilled through an alembic, and 6 ounces of rectified oil, and mix these together—namely 6 ounces [of the substance]—onto <unclear>pure wool</unclear> <note>original: "pira lanna." Possibly referring to a filter or a specific mineral consistency.</note> upon a <term>porphyry</term> <note>A very hard purple-red stone used as a slab for grinding and mixing alchemical ingredients.</note> little by little, or upon some other slab, until it drinks it all up. And when it has absorbed everything, leave it in a closed glass vessel for 8 days. On the eighth day, place this charge in an alembic, having provided the three necessary waters. Put it in the housing and distill it so many times that you do not breathe <note>Likely an instruction to keep the apparatus hermetically sealed so no vapors escape.</note>, and place it in the <term>horse's belly</term> <note>original: "ventre equi." A common alchemical term for a heap of fermenting horse manure, which provided a steady, gentle heat for digestion.</note> to digest for 15 days. On the sixteenth day, you will find the plates dissolved in a water as white as milk, changed in the dung from the rotation on the fourth day. And this is my physical water; indeed, it is a physical oil and a penetrating water. It holds all burning candles and illuminates all things; it can be sustained by itself alone, and it is retained with this water.

<summary>This page provides a detailed recipe for "Physical Water," a potent alchemical solvent made from distilled sal ammoniac and rectified oil, aged in the heat of a manure heap.</summary>
<keywords>sal ammoniac, physical water, distillation, horse's belly, alchemy, universal solvent, water of life</keywords>
